<!-- Added 2026-11-24 from MIT 18.03SC Differential Equations (Fall 2011), Session 38 (limit cycles, showing they exist and do not exist, the Van der Pol equation, chaos), in our own wording; examples are ours, checked in Python; proofs written out in steps. The Poincaré–Bendixson theorem is stated without proof, as in the course. -->

Linear systems have closed orbits only in families around a center. Nonlinear systems can have an *isolated* closed orbit that attracts its neighbours, a self-sustained oscillation. In three dimensions even stranger behaviour, chaos, appears.

::: {#def-limit-cycle .definition name="Limit cycle"}
A *limit cycle* of an autonomous system is a closed trajectory $C$ (a periodic solution) such that nearby trajectories spiral towards $C$ or away from it as $t\to\infty$. It is *stable* if trajectories on both sides approach it, *unstable* if they recede on both sides, and *semistable* otherwise.
:::

::: {#exm-polar-limit-cycle .example name="A limit cycle in polar coordinates"}
The system $r'=r(1-r^2)$, $\theta'=1$ (in polar coordinates) has the circle $r=1$ as a trajectory. For $0<r<1$, $r'>0$, and for $r>1$, $r'<0$; so every trajectory except the origin spirals to the circle, which is a stable limit cycle. In Cartesian form the system is $x'=x-y-x(x^2+y^2)$, $y'=x+y-y(x^2+y^2)$.
:::

```

Trajectories of $r'=r(1-r^2)$, $\theta'=1$ spiral onto the stable limit cycle $r=1$ (thick) from inside and outside.

:::

::: {#thm-poincare-bendixson .theorem name="Poincaré–Bendixson"}
Let $R$ be a closed bounded region of the plane containing no critical point of a $2\times2$ autonomous system with continuously differentiable $f,g$. If a trajectory enters $R$ and stays in it for all later time, then either it is a closed trajectory or it spirals towards a closed trajectory in $R$.
:::

::: {#thm-bendixson-criterion .theorem name="Bendixson's criterion"}
If $f_x+g_y$ is continuous and has constant sign (never zero) on a simply connected region $R$, then the system $x'=f$, $y'=g$ has no closed trajectory lying entirely in $R$.
:::

::: {.proof}
1. Suppose $C$ is a closed trajectory in $R$, traversed once counterclockwise in time $P$, enclosing the region $D\subseteq R$.

2. Along $C$, $dx=f\,dt$ and $dy=g\,dt$, so $\oint_C(f\,dy-g\,dx)=\int_0^P(fg-gf)\,dt=0$.

3. By Green's theorem (@thm-greens-theorem) with $M=-g$, $N=f$, the same integral equals $\iint_D(f_x+g_y)\,dA$.

4. That double integral is nonzero because the integrand has constant sign on $D$, a contradiction.
:::

::: {#exm-van-der-pol .example name="The Van der Pol oscillator"}
The equation $x''+\mu(x^2-1)x'+x=0$ with $\mu>0$ models an electric oscillator with negative damping at small amplitude and positive damping at large amplitude. As the system $x'=y$, $y'=-x-\mu(x^2-1)y$:

1. the only critical point $(0,0)$ has $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&\mu\end{pmatrix}$, $T=\mu>0$, $\Delta=1$: an unstable spiral or node, so trajectories leave it;
2. far from the origin the damping is positive and trajectories move inwards;
3. it is a classical theorem (proved with the ideas of @thm-poincare-bendixson) that there is exactly one stable limit cycle; numerically, for $\mu=1$ its amplitude in $x$ is about $2$.

Bendixson's criterion is consistent with this: $f_x+g_y=-\mu(x^2-1)$ changes sign on the lines $x=\pm1$, which the cycle must cross.
:::

```

The Van der Pol oscillator with $\mu=1$: a trajectory starting near the unstable origin and two starting far out all approach the same limit cycle (thick), of amplitude about $2$ in $x$.

:::

::: {#rmk-chaos .remark name="Chaos"}
Poincaré–Bendixson rules out chaotic behaviour in the plane: bounded trajectories end at critical points or closed orbits. In three dimensions this fails. The *Lorenz system* $x'=\sigma(y-x)$, $y'=x(\rho-z)-y$, $z'=xy-\beta z$ with $\sigma=10$, $\rho=28$, $\beta=8/3$ has bounded trajectories that never settle down and depend sensitively on the initial point: two starts $10^{-8}$ apart separate to the size of the attractor within a few dozen time units. This *sensitive dependence on initial conditions* is the mark of chaos.
:::

::: {#fig-limit-cycles-and-chaos-3}
